#352885 955 2007-08-09T02:12:04Z incluye

#353051 962 2007-08-09T15:21:05Z incluye
Quote: T3chno, August 09, 2007 05:11 am
Quote: 321southpaw, August 09, 2007 04:26 am
Quote: incluye, August 09, 2007 01:59 am
okay, this is driving me nuts.  How do you guys make those backgrounds?  I got the pictures of Sora just fine.  But I can't make the backgrounds.
Just PM me with some help.  Or tell me how.  Better yet, give me a step-by-step guide.
Step 1: Go to deviantArt.com

Step 2: Find a brush set that says "Abstract" somewhere within the title

Step 3: Download and add to your brushes folder

Step 4: Experiment

Step 5: Practice patience. You might get a good background on your first try, but the next ten will be more difficult, and then they will get easier (I approximated the numbers, but you get my gist)

That is really all there is to it. Imaging is all about practice, patience, and persistence.
Oh yes. He basically summed it all up there. And once you get the hand of brushes, try different styles of putting them in. For example, fill your 1/2 up with the brushes you want. Then invert the whole thing, and apply the same brushes. They will look different. Mess around with the opacity of brushes as well, as some brushes do come in a very dark color.
Two things:
1. I can't find what you guys are talking about on deviantART. Where do I go on the site?
2. Is that stuff for Photoshop?

Okay, figured it out. How do I use new brushes with GIMP?

Quote: Turtle, August 03, 2007 07:32 am
Well, Im just about on the younger side of the general majority of you guys. Not like under twelve, but I am at the age that people find it cool to go out with one another. For me, the concept of 'cool' isn't exactly appealing. The only thing I want dealing with a social life is a group of good friends who would never and could never back out on me at the last second. Like that would ever happen. I am a NERD. I can never deny that. I get straight A's, I take classes higher than anyone else in my year, and the only class I have gotten a B in in any of the quarters was P.E, and although that was because of a mathematical error, it still is on my record. I have never gone out with anyone, nor have I been asked. Im not sure if it's a good thing or not. I sorta want to laugh at the people who are going out, yet I feel a curious jealous feeling pulling at me. But the horrible thing is the expectations put on me. I know I dont want to work my hardest. I know it deep down to the bone. I only like working if I am interested in the topic, and now, I am losing my interest in the thing that is the most vital for me. That is math. I am three years above what I am supposed to be in math, and I am taking the class as an honors course. So basically, I will need to spend day and night on something I dont want to work on. And I will be expected to get an A. I know I can do it, it's just that Im afraid I wont work hard enough. I never do. It's really weird.

Apart from all of that school jibber-jabber, there is my actual personality. Like I said, all I want are friends who I can trust, and who can trust me with their lives. I actually probably have a couple of those. The more that people know me, the more they seem to like me. Although, that said, no girl would ever dare ask me out, because if they do, their social life will be down the drain. I am at the bottom of the social circle. Either way, my personality seems to be good. All who know me seem to think I am kind. I am very religious, but I am not a Christian. Nor a Muslim. Nor a Hindu. I am not Buddhist. I am a Baha'i, a religion that is not known very well. I spend hours of my weeks doing Baha'i related tasks, such as teaching children's classes and praying and reading Baha'i books. Laugh if you want, but I have really PRAYED for some of you. Not really for a reason, but because you seemed down, or something. Either way, that is that.

And then there is my family life. That couldnt be going much better. My mom and dad are perfectly happy with each other. My brother and I are perfeclty happy with each other (in a different way), but my brother will be going off to college pretty soon, which will be a big blow to me. Oh well. I only have a couple of years after him. Either way, if I was to grade my life, it would look like this: Social life: F. School (work) life: A-. Personality: A-. Religion: A. Family:A. So, off of that, I will average it out at an A-, considering I really dont care about the social thing. My life is good, and I think that so long as you can still keep standing, or at least still keep fighting for what you believe in, deep down, you can make it no matter what.

Wow...
Anyway.
I'm almost exactly like Turtle, as some people may have picked up.
I won a school spelling bee and was in honors classes, not that I'm bragging, just stating facts.
I've been playing soccer for seven years and basketball for one, and I've been building DDA's for a few months. Every one of them has been top rated at one time or another.
I get straight A's, except when I have trouble procrastinating, which is happening less and less as I'm going to an advanced school program called PEP.
I play cello and piano.
I rollerblade.
I write stories. (anywhere from 60 to 350 Word pages)
Many people I know from public school say I'm good at everything, but I feel like I'm not. I've met at least one person who's better in something than me. In fact, I've met people who are better than me at everything up there. I know someone who can write stories really well. One of my best friends is on the premier soccer team. Et cetera.
My big problem is that I always feel like I am the best one at a certain thing until I meet somebody who's better, and then I'm all down in the dumps because I feel like I'm sub-average.
Reading this thread has helped, though, as I see some of the stuff that happens to people, Suki in particular. Man, I thought I had it bad.
I'm on the far side of thirteen, which is an iffy physical/mental/emotional state of life, and I'm really apprehensive as to what's coming.

#356992 21 2007-08-19T05:43:13Z incluye
Name: Shock Drone
Abilities: Crawls very slowly on a surface, like a drone. Whenever it sees N on the surface it's on, it starts up a brief charging period similar to a Laser Drone's then shocks the entire surface it's crawling on. If N is wallsliding on, standing on, or touching the surface, he gets fried.
Appearance: A darker, more greenish blue than zap drones. Possibly a black triangle pointing toward the surface it's crawling on. This triangle would turn red when the drone was charging, then transparent as it zapped.
Why it should be in N: In my opinion it would be more fun to have an enemy like this. It could be adjusted based on the surface area it's shocking, and it would add another level to the gameplay.
Plus it'd be awesome in DDAs.

#17136 1 2007-09-08T17:27:35Z incluye
Attention! Attention!

I have decided to recruit for a new type of map pack: a tileset pack. The object is to make excellent tilesets: never want for a tileset again, just grab one from the pack and build a level.
Once the mappack is completed, it will be open for people to use. If you want to make a level from any tileset, just ask.

The name of this pack is Mosaicist--I've decided to call it Mosaicism instead so those who make tilesets are known as the Mosaicists. And here's the sig bar for the members, thanks to Animator:

Please post in this thread with your intents for joining, and the number of tilesets you expect to make. You must name each of your tilesets.

Post them in userlevels format in a .txt file. I will compile them into one big .txt file, which can be found here as levels accumulate.

You may submit one or two tilesets from NUMA, but I want the majority of maps to be completely original.

If you submit a map from a tileset in this pack, put a link to this thread on the level description.

Regards,
incluye


Members:
Me
kkstrong0
gloomp
The_apprentice
Animator

#363465 48 2007-09-09T03:39:53Z incluye
Quote: blue_tetris, September 09, 2007 02:36 am
Quote: Turtle, September 08, 2007 09:16 pm
Quote: suki, 2007-09-08T14:29:02Z
I'm going to torture you for 60 years. I'm going to strap you to a chair and induce the worst pain you could possibly feel, for sixty continuous years. I'll toss in more than just physical pain, too. I'll let you meet people, and I'll let you watch them betray you. I'll make you fall in love and have your heart broken. I'll make you feel powerless. I'll make you feel hated. I'll break you physically and emotionally, and mercilessly, and repeatedly. For sixty solid years. And just when you think you've seen the worst, I'll make you know that that wasn't even the beginning.
But then I'm gonna give you a billion dollars.
What do ya say? 

wait a minute...
are you comparing a billion dollars with heaven?
Suki has a problem with hyperbolizing in the wrong direction. Not a good way to make a point to someone who disagrees with him.

The better analogy is this:

If a guy struck you with a stick one day and gave you $100 the next, you might be quick to think he's benevolent. But God should be omnibenevolent. He is the whole of all that is good. If a conscious entity wants to inflict pain, then later wants to inflict great pleasure, she can be somewhat benevolent. But she cannot be omnibenevolent. (Assuming that benevolence requires preventing suffering, if you can.) If I torture and berate the members of IRC one day, and enjoy games of Worms and party down with them the next day; I can be a lot of things, but I cannot be morally-minded. At least not all the time.



Here's the argument broken down:

God is all-powerful. He can do anything. Senseless suffering is bad. Senseless suffering occurs. God has the power to end it. God does not. God is not benevolent, because God has wittingly allowed bad to occur.

(or)

God is not all-powerful. He can do a lot, but cannot do everything. Senseless suffering is bad. God does what He can to end it, but He ultimately cannot. God is omnibenevolent, because He has done everything in His power to end suffering.



God has some ultimate plans for people, and some of His followers (like some of you guys) want to talk about them sometimes. Why are there nearly a billion people who are never given the chance to know God and experience great suffering in life? Why do these people exist? Why does God allow them to continue in this state?

Three big questions for theists to answer:

Does God have the power to end senseless suffering (suffering that does not teach anything to those enduring it)?
Does God have the will to end senseless suffering?
If God has both the power and will to end senseless suffering, why does it exist?

The Answers:
God has the power to end senseless suffering.
God has the will to end senseless suffering.
It exists because he's giving us a choice. For example, if you only had one path to go, would you really be "choosing it?"
No.
If the only path was to love God, would it be real love? Would you really love something you loved only because you were forced to?
No.
I may appear stubborn, but I believe in God no matter what other issues are raised.

#18167 1 2007-11-28T02:54:40Z incluye
Don't worry, folks, I won't be leaving in the middle of the compilation.


So, write a chapter, make a map.


Yes, you read this correctly. I want people to help me write a book. The levels will be released on NUMA with the chapter in the level description.
Why?



Post in the thread to sign up. I'll make a list of every member, in order. When it's your turn (like the forum worm) you will have up to five days to write a chapter and make your map. If you think that's an awful short time, let me know. If you think it's far too long, tell me.

Once you've written the chapter, post it in the same .txt file as your map. The next person will read the chapter and add their own, continuing the storyline.

I will be the master editor for the writing. Maps, too, will be judged--if your map is rejected, you have the choice whether to make another or not. I would like two people to help me judge, and we can fill in if anyone's map is not accepted and they don't want to make another one.

Acceptable level types: action, race, puzzle, tileset.

If possible, I would like it if the novel is sort of about N's personal life outside his frantic enemy-dodging job.

Good luck.


~incluye

1. Pheidippides (possibly)

#385119 59 2007-12-01T21:21:16Z incluye
Well, I actually chose "incluir" at first because that's an infinitive which [I thought] means "to create." After awhile I decided I didn't want people thinking it was pronounced "incloyer" or "inclooyer" or whatever, and I changed it to the el/ella/Usted form "incluye." Shortly afterwards, I discovered that "incluir" does not mean "to create." It means "to include."

I should have seen that coming.

#19456 1 2008-03-10T02:52:56Z incluye
Well, the last idea was a bust, and Pheidi has inspired me in Transcend. If you don't want me copying your idea, dude, just say the word.
Spoiler: Part 1||Chapter 1N bit his lip as the bullet bit the ground. He could feel the heat from the rocket behind him.
He landed like a cat on a sloped tile, ducked and cursed as a laser beam shot past him, then leaped off the tile, clearing a distance equal to four times his height. He grabbed the edge of a bounceblock and pulled himself up.
As he felt a slight pressure under his foot, a ring of trapdoors closed around the exit. N cursed again, loudly, and slipped off the bounceblock, landing on the laser drone below him. The drone hummed loudly in protest. Seconds later, N felt it heat up.
He had seconds.
N soared off the drone, executed a fluid double backflip, and dug his hands and feet into one of the trapdoors. He could feel, rather than see, a laser dot on his back. He walljumped upward and snatched onto the edge of a one-way platform, just as the drone opened fire.
The 3500˚C+ temperature of the laser was enough to melt through the trapdoor. N whirled as he heard the thump-thump-thump of chaingun bullets slamming into the ground. With superhuman reflexes, he snatched one of the bullets out of the air, dodged the rest, then plummeted, smashing the bullet through the laser drone's hard outer shell.
It crackled and whined. N could feel it heat up, and he positioned the gaping hole in the direction of the chaingun.
A wave of heat, a loud zap, and the chaingun hissed, buzzed, and crashed to the tiles below it.
N leaped onto the bounceblock, then double-jumped off of it, grabbing the edge of the cooling gap in the trapdoor. He pulled himself through, ready to depress the exit switch, leave this level, and finally achieve transcendence.
He saw a flash of color in the corner of his eye. N whirled, just as the thing disappeared below a tile. Exit forgotten, he jogged over and looked down. His heart stopped.
There, on a locked door, sat a blue ninja in the lotus position. N could tell it was a female-and exceptionally beautiful.
She looked up, saw him, and was gone.
Spoiler: Part 1||Chapter 2He woke up.
It was 4 o'clock in the morning. N lay quietly in bed. Ever since he had taken up a life of chaos and death, he had had recurring bad dreams, and last night was no different. He remembered the hundreds of chainguns, thwumps raining from the sky, and, of course, N's nemesis Espada777777. N and Espada were twins, separated at birth. And while N fought for justice, Espada fought for chaos. Neither had ever met the other, but both had heard the legends of their brother.

N got out of bed, poured himself a coffee, turned on the computer, and opened his message service. "You have one new message," mumbled the computer in blank voice. N opened the message. "Assignment Number 231," he read, and sighed at the thought.
"For twelve years now, a ninja known as Espada777777 has caused destruction and despair." Shocked, N opened his eyes wide. "We have found his secret lair, and need you to infiltrate it. You must execute Espada and save a close friend of ours who he has kidnapped."
N was curious to who this person was. He read on.
"Her name is Lepidoptera. She is a highly skilled blue ninja as well as an acclaimed master of sabotage."
"Who is this blue ninja?" N thought to himself. The message went on.
"We believe that Lepidoptera has been brainwashed, and we now have evidence to believe that she is hidden in Espada's lair."
For a moment, his mind was blank.
Then with a shock, N remembered that fateful evening five long years ago. The blue ninja that disappeared leapt unbidden to his mind. He recalled his endless searches for her. A red surge of hatred for his treacherous twin boiled in his chest, but he held it in.
"You must do this, N," commanded the message, "for the good of the country, the world, the whole of ninja kind and Lepidoptera. Do you accept?"
The email ended. N sat for a few seconds, pondering.
He hit the reply button. "Yes."
(this chapter by sneekfeet)
Spoiler: Part 1||Chapter 3N sat in the helicopter, downdraft from the blades whipping his nonexistent hair around his nonexistent face. He leaned out the open side and looked down at the cement below him. Then he looked up at the pilot, who was watching him, headphones and microphone positioned oddly on his triangular head.
N gave him a thumbs-up silently, and they lifted off as the door hatch slid into place.
N donned his own headset and adjusted the mike, adjusting the dial on the side for frequency. He found the pilot's and sat back in the seat, thinking.
"Kid," crackled the pilot's voice through the ancient headphones. "We'll be there in about four hours. Espada's lair is one of the most dangerous places on earth, so I'm gonna give you cover fire for about 300 yards. Then you're on your own."
N sighed and tilted his head back. 'Kid'? Some kind of official operation this was. He looked back at the pilot.
"What do you know about Lepidoptera and Espada?" he asked.
"Not much. All I know is, I was ferrying Lepidoptera back to the HQ. She said she had some incriminating pictures, I dunno what that meant. Then, suddenly, there was some kinda electric pulse, and I lost control of the chopper. We were about eighty feet above the helipad, and we both jumped as the copter fell. I came out fine, but I think Lepidoptera was hit with some debris and got knocked out. Next thing I know, there's someone pressing a gun to my back. I see Lepidoptera picked up by a ninja in black and get carried away, and the gun guy goes after them. I climb down to HQ and tell everyone what happened, and naturally they called you, N."
"You didn't even try to rescue her yourself?"
The pilot turned around in his seat and looked at N for a long time. Presently he said, "Kid, I've learned a lot of things in my life as a soldier. First thing I learned was, if you can't do it, don't try. Works marvelously."
N felt fury building in his chest. "You chose your own skin over another ninja's. What kind of soldier are you?"
The pilot turned back and looked out the front window. "Kid, you don't antagonize a pilot. I'm liable to drop you off right here if you don't stop trash-talk..."
"What's wrong?" asked N.
A beam of heat sheared through the window, blasting pieces off the rotor and melting into the engine. The helicopter died as the pilot wrestled furiously with the controls. They began to plummet.
"Laser drone," screamed the pilot. "Gotta be a damn dozen of 'em. Help me!"
"I can't fly a copter!"
"You're supposed to be a ninja! Get me outta here! I don't wanna die!" The pilot's voice rose to a shrill scream.
N stared at him. "Save you? After what you said? No thanks, bub. I'm out."
He strapped on his parachute and turned to leap from the broken window.
He couldn't do it.
He had to save the pilot.
N turned back and grabbed the pilot with one strong arm. "Hold on tight, I don't know if this thing can hold both of us."
Another laser flashed through N's field of vision, and he leapt from the helicopter's cockpit as it exploded.
They fell toward the desert.
Spoiler: Part 1||Chapter 4A laser flashed inches above N's head and he heard his parachute rip as it caught fire. He grabbed the pilot to help slow their descent.
They smashed into a sand dune. N let out a grunt of pain as his leg twisted under him. He reached out and felt the pilot's pulse--stable, but faint, and growing fainter.
He gritted his teeth against the pain and lifted the pilot, limping through the dunes until he tripped on a protruding stone. Lasers exploding around him, N fell into the dank darkness of an underground cave.
He regained his footing as he landed, looking around, eyes opened as wide as he could open them in vain search for light. He felt around for the pilot and located the unconscious ninja, hoisting him again over his shoulders.
Which way to go? he thought, swiveling his head and sniffing the air.
Then he saw it--a faint, almost nonexistent flicker in the opposite direction of the cave mouth.
Light.
With only a moment's hesitation, he looked back, then limped off toward the flicker, carrying the injured pilot on his back.

Welcome, Perimetric.

As a beginner, it's alright to use jump-pads in your DDA's, but as you get more experienced, you should rather go without them and find other methods of movin' N around. The same applies to gold delay. Also, you should try not have too many unnecessary objects as the load time is uber long.

Of course, though, you'll get better at map-making (and especially DDA's if that's the direction you're going). Next time you want your maps noticed, it's always a good idea to post them at the Map-Rating Thread. You'll probably get more attention there. And if you're looking for a list of some of the best DDA's ever made, click this. It may help.

Ironically, Mae, the first DDA on that list is infested with launchpads--"The Improbability Drive."

Anyway, Perimetric (nice name, dude), what I always try to do in any DDA is keep N in the center of the action, and not use gold delay/launchpads.

#416539 2 2008-03-13T16:43:52Z incluye
Strengths:
-I like my gameplay pretty well.
-I make usable, small tilesets.
-Good enemy placement.
-Oh yeah, and DDAs.

Weaknesses:
-Entire-screen action maps. I can't sit down and map, just like you, Nexx. Recently T3chno told me that he makes his tilesets by scattering random 5-tiles, then connecting them.
-No-jump actions. My no-jump maps would cause you nausea.
-Using gauss/chainguns well.
-Gold placement. I just can't make an extra challenge with the gold, like so many mappers (Pheidi, Skyline, etc.) seem to be able to.
-Races.

When I started making maps in N, I gradually realized I wasn't any good at playable maps. I found the tiles artistic and easy to work with, so I started making tilesets. I also quickly discovered that I found DDAs easy and fun to make.
Recently, I've started to make more action maps. (My last four maps were action, I think.) From IRCing to MNNML, I'm becoming inspired.
My races are not a thing of beauty. I just can't grasp the flow, like in_dub and Bunnies can. The race level, for me, is difficult to make and unrewarding. I would definitely appreciate race tips from anyone who's good at them.

A T3chnological experiment, in which I tried to make his fancy backgrounds in the GIMP. GIMP is lacking several filters, though, so I put the background through about fifteen others before giving up, oilifying, then shifting the image horizontally and vertically. I don't remember exactly what I did, though.

Render: Leonidas from 300 (taken from PlanetRenders.net)
Main colors: Um...black. And dark grey. A tiny amount of teal/aquamarine.
Brushes used: Lightning Brushes by Aggr3ssi0n. Can be found here.
Filters: Apply Canvas, Shift, Oilify, Ripple, Mosaic, Erode, Motion Blur, Add Noise, a few others I don't remember.
Size: 400x200.

#419837 61 2008-03-22T01:07:04Z incluye
A time ago, I used to be very concerned about ratings. This got me into trouble.
kisler, you would not have seen this.
Anyway, I submitted what I still think is my best DDA, Final Showdown, a chaingun/gauss-based map. Shortly after submitting it, it had a 4.5 average rating by 7 or so people and was the top-rated map. Shortly after this, I went to Canada.
When I came back, it was 4 by 24 people and bitesized.
Partially due to my inflated ego and higher expectations of ratings, I thought, "Oh NOES! It has been SNIPED!"
Disillusioned waif I, I proceeded to delete and resubmit it.
We came home.
When I looked at it back home, I looked at it. 4 by 19 people and bitesized. ATOB had bitesized it again, encountering critcism from other members for his decision. Frogs had this particularly descriptive line: "Cry me a ****ing river."
MaximumRide (what a great guy) eventually told me, "Just delete it. It shows you don't care."
Eventually I deleted it. I have no idea if that data is still there somewhere.

Now when I see a snipe, I think, "Tough." When Chase was sniped from top-rated to the bowels of NUMA, I thought, "Tough."
It works very well.

Listen to Turtle. She be a smart amphibian.

Quote: blue_tetris, May 27, 2008 02:01 am
The focus of the Nmaps.net is to show you the newest and the best.  No one cares to see the best map of all time--there's no way to decide what that is using a rating system like this for over 100,000 maps.  People want to see the newest maps that have been rated highly.  That's what Hot Maps is about.
Wait a moment, please. As far as I can see, Hot Maps doesn't work that way. At all. In fact, I'm going to go see what the Hottest Map is on Newma right now.
...
...
...
Odd. The Hottest Map is called "chooser" by "flukey123." It appears and plays noobishly. The next three are triplicates of another noobish map called "no enemies" by "lord_doom_gx." I see only three maps with any ratings on here, and they're all near the bottom.
Would the Hottest Map not be "Capita X" by Losttortuga, a very highly rated (5 by 5) tileset? Wouldn't it stay the hottest until another map bumped it off? I see no maps even close to such prestige. Face it, people. Maybe there are bumps to be ironed out, but Hot Maps is not the Hot Maps page. It's the Newest page under a different name.

Naturally, of course, in Oldma "Capita X" would zoom straight to the top-rated page, where it would get rated more and possibly bitesized. But now the only way people will see it is by word of mouth and by looking up Losttortuga directly.

Besides, I DO want to see the greatest maps of all time. It's entirely plausible that I'm the only member in the entire Metanet community that can watch a DDA more than once or look at an N-art more than once. But Afternoondragon Step 04 was unarguably AMAZING (at least, according to the 1006 votes). MTI was amazing, and a brilliant challenge. On Route 666 was just unbelievable. *takes a random map* Sleeping with a Fish Named Eric was addictive, fun, pleasing to the eye, and deserving of its spot. But hey, I'm not gonna see those again, not unless I specifically search for them.

I've got many problems with Newma. But that's one of the big ones.

#440627 5 2008-05-31T00:19:28Z incluye
Quote: peace_bear0, May 30, 2008 11:34 pm
  1. Can we collab also?
  2. Can we take tiles/enemies from someone else? (I can't make tiles nor enemies very well)
  3. Do we get one point for each type of propulsion we use?
  4. How do you make glitched tiles/ tile propulsion?
  5. How do we submit them?

1. Yes.
2. You can take tiles. Enemies? Why would you need to take enemies from someone else?
3. Yes.
4. Tile propulsion is towerloop style, as in squeezing N between tiles to make him go really fast. Glitched tiles are a * in the leveldata in the place of a normal tile. (* may be incorrect, in that case ask someone else, because that's what I thought it was) So, for example, to use your tile, you put a full tile in the place you want it, go to your leveldata, and change the 1 into a *.
5. Stick them in a text file and post them on here. I'll allow NUMA submissions. In that case, post the link.

#20501 1 2008-06-11T23:50:06Z incluye
So here it is.
People do a lot of collabs, and collab accounts are made, etc. I don't think this will really be that useful, but my idea is that there should be a system for crediting maps to two people.
E.g., when you submit a map, there should be a check box that says "This is a collab" (rather like that which says, "Disable ratings on this map.")
If you check it, you then type in the other member's name in a box below the check box or find it in a dropdown list. When you click "Submit," the server will send a message to the user in question, asking them to approve the collab request.
If they do, the map will be credited to both users. It will show in both of their galleries and, when someone views the map, it'll say both users' names by "Author."

"And I say also this. - Hyoi

#445753 523 2008-06-17T22:44:39Z incluye
Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-17T17:51:24Z
...because creationists frequently claim that 1. Mutations can never be beneficial
On the contrary, beneficial mutations can occur. However, as far as I know, they're usually small and more or less insignificant.

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-17T17:51:24Z
2. that genetic information always decreases.
That was confusing, when I read it at first. After considering it for a few seconds, I figured you meant "that useful genetic information always decreases." It doesn't. There's a significant difference between "always decreases" and "never increases." Even accounting for the production of new enzymes, I think (ignorantly, probably enough) that the emergence of nylonase is an adaptation that allows it to more suitably live in its environment.

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-17T17:51:24Z
Whether or not the bacteria would change back, if moved to an environment with nylon, I don't know. I don't see how it's particularly relevant, either.
Well, precisely my point. If the nylonase lost its ability to metabolize nylon byproducts after being removed from an environment containing them, I would see that as its ability being an adaptation as opposed to an example of evolution.

Quote: Strife, 2008-06-17T21:14:45Z
Honestly, arguing against evolution in this day and age is like arguing that balls can't bounce while the opposing sides play a game of tennis.
If evolution really has prevailed, why are we (Creationists) able to keep arguing? Convincingly, no less?

In any case, I'd like to start an argument instead of just defending my position against others.

The emergence of DNA. DNA consists of four different chemicals arranged in complicated strings. About six feet of DNA exists in each of our bodies' one hundred trillion cells. For perspective, the information needed to build the proteins for all the species of organisms ever lived could be held in a teaspoon, leaving room for all the information in every book ever written.
A number of processes do occur in nature - the creation of salt one. Sodium and chloride ions bond naturally due to chemical attractions. However, they simply make a sequence of NaClNaClNaClNaCl over and over and over. DNA is entirely "random." Non-random DNA would not hold the information necessary to create life.
Therefore, the origin of DNA is unexplainable by natural selection (as far as I know.) The super-complicatedness of DNA, along with its randomness (usually, especially at DNA's magnitude, very unlikely in nature), seems to beg some kind of explanation other than pure chance.
Comprehensible information always comes from intelligence. Even writing a comprehensible sentence with totally random letters is ultraimprobable. It seems logical to infer that some kind of creator was involved in the making of DNA.

#445975 532 2008-06-18T17:28:28Z incluye
Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-18T16:40:39Z
That's a false distinction. Adaptation is a word you'll hear a lot, if, you know, you take a biology class or something. Adaptation is a word used by every evolutionary biologist since Darwin. But, see, nylon-eating bacteria are only one mutation different from the bacteria that they came from. Undoing one mutation is unlikely, but it will probably happen if you give it enough time and evolutionary pressure. But let's say the nylon-eating population mutates again... and again. The chance that these mutations will be undone as well as nylonase grow exponentially smaller. Eventually, after enough mutations, the evolutionary pressure could change, but it would be essentially impossible for the bacteria to change back into what they once were because they would have too many mutations to undo.
Nylonase is a bacterium. It can metabolize nylon. But it's still a bacterium. Are mutations ever - and I mean ever, in absolutely any circumstances - going to significantly advance it to a higher life form? Is the bacteria ever going to evolve into, say, a plankton, through countless mutations? (Many mutations are harmful or even deadly to the organism.)

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-18T16:40:39Z
See my stone arch argument from "Why do you believe what you believe?" If that's not satisfying (and it probably won't be), then I'll do a full reply. Right now, though, I don't want to go to the trouble since I've recently made a relevant argument.
Yeah, maybe there was some kind of "scaffold" for amino acids to assemble themselves. But as far as we know, it hasn't been discovered yet. I think that's a fairly important discovery to have, because without it, evolutionists cannot explain the emergence of DNA. It's like the fossil record - you (evolutionists) keep saying that more fossil discoveries will support your theory, but they're not. They're contradicting it. E.g., the Cambrian explosion. (I'll cover that in a minute.)

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-18T16:40:39Z
Evolution is *not* pure chance. It would not work if it were random, and no reputable biologist would claim that evolution is random. The entire point is that there is non-random selection in nature. If creatures lived and died randomly, they could never improve or adapt to their surroundings, because the organisms that are less fit would have an equal chance of surviving and producing offspring. Evolution, instead of claiming that selection is based on pure chance, claims that organisms live and die because they were fit enough to survive or they were not fit enough to survive.
Point taken. But you took that quote out of context. I was referring to DNA there, whereas you're applying my "pure chance" phrase to evolution in general. What I don't understand is how organisms could have become stronger and more advanced through relatively random mutations. I find minor improvements - e.g., nylonase - plausible, but not major ones.

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-18T16:40:39Z
He interviewed one astronomer, and Michael Behe (who doesn't actually count). Every other person he interviewed was not a scientist in the relevant field, except maybe the philosopher of science who talked about the relationship between science and religion.

Jonathan Wells is a PhD in Molecular and Cellular Biology.
Stephen C. Meyer has a degree in Geology.
Don't they count?

Quote: Demonz, 2008-06-18T16:40:39Z
Well, I addressed what you said about DNA. Maybe it would help if you talked about *why* the Cambrian explosion is evidence against evolution.
Very well.

After googling the term "Cambrian Explosion," I find that it is "the seemingly rapid appearance of most major groups of complex animals around 530 million years ago, as evidenced by the fossil record." It took about 5 million years, and during it, a huge amount of multi-celled, complex organisms appeared.

Some scientists propose a process called "punctuated equilibrium," a theory I find to be implausible, explaining that small groups of animals evolved very quickly, leaving no intermediate stages in the fossil record.

Other scientists posit that such rapid evolution cannot have occurred in such a short time.

Four blondes and a brunette, for some reason, are dangling by a spindly rope from the spire of the Eiffel Tower.
Soon, the rope begins to unravel, and the four realizes that one of them will have to sacrifice herself to save the others.
The brunette, being a noble soul, composes a long and touching speech on the spot, about how she will preserve blonde rights and save the lives of her comrades by falling to her death.
Astounded at her loquacity, the blondes applaud.

---

A blonde, a brunette, and a redhead have died in a car accident and are ascending the stairs to heaven.
An angel appears to them and says, "I am going to tell you a joke. If you laugh, you will be sent to Hell." He tells the joke.
The redhead, unfortunately, laughs. A trapdoor opens beneath her feet and she plummets to Hell.
Another angel appears and says the same thing. This time the brunette laughs and falls to Hell.
The blonde mounts the remaining steps to Heaven. Suddenly, she starts cracking up.
"What's so funny?" God asks.
"I just got the first joke!"

---

Once upon a time, there were two lightning bolts - Mother Lightning and her son, Jimmy Lightning.
One day, a tornado struck in Abilene, Texas, and Mother Lightning was conscripted by the Storm Authorities to assist in the making of chaos there. Before departing, she told Jimmy: "Stay in our cloud, and don't go anywhere else! The world is a dangerous place for a collection of positively charged ions. But I know you're very bright, so you'll stay out of trouble."
Being mischievous, however, Jimmy began to explore the skies as soon as his Mother Lightning left for Texas. Soon he was aspiring to leap down to earth and explore there, for he heard the city lights were marvelous at nighttime.
Fated as he was for doom, Jimmy Lightning fell from his cloud and struck the earth, accidentally channeling himself directly into a lightning rod. He was sucked into the ground and neutralized by the negative ions there.
Spoiler: The Moral.Do what your parents say, or else you might get grounded.
---

An Englishman, a Frenchman, a Texan, and a Mexican (as well as a pilot) are on a trip on a small plane.
The engine suddenly makes a horrendous grinding noise, and the pilot loses the controls. Wrestling furiously with them, he shouts, "Only one passenger is gonna survive this! Three of you have to jump out!"
The Englishman shouts, "Long Live the Queen!" and leaps from the now open cabin door, plummeting to his death.
The Frenchman yells, "Vive le France!" and jumps from the plane.
The Texan, becoming excited and inspired, screams, "Remember the Alamo!"
He seizes the Mexican and throws him out of the plane.

So we're kind of at an impasse; according to our laws of physics, the Big Bang couldn't have happened, and we don't have any evidence that there were no laws of physics to stop it.
You're very quick to make these kinds of statements, incluye. Y'know, the kinds that say, "well, looks by [my convoluted interpretation of the discussion] that we could both be right or wrong; best stop arguing and continue doing what we do."
While that might be the case on a number of issues, this isn't one of them. Do you remember when I brought up COBE? If you read it, or even skimmed it, how could you say that a current scientific theory is scientifically implausible?

Strife: I'm not saying that the Big Bang didn't happen. In fact, I'm pro-Big Bang. I'm saying it didn't just randomly occur - my point actually is that God caused it to happen.

Quote: player_03, 2008-06-26T18:12:56Z
I'm not sure why (it's complicated, and I have better things to do than trying to understand it), but scientists have drawn up charts of the four fundamental forces that show them as having been combined in the beginning.
Well...that's just a chart. I can make a chart about how all birds were once rocks, but that doesn't mean it'll be true.

Quote: player_03, 2008-06-26T18:12:56Z
Also, just because something sounds weird/wrong doesn't mean it isn't true. For example, objects can recede from one another at faster than the speed of light without actually breaking any laws of physics (an explanation of this can be found here).
Again, I don't really have a response to that. I can't understand the theory at the moment, but it sounds more or less reasonable.

But picking up from the actual webpage:
"We dont say that things are moving apart faster than light. Imagine that they are all sitting still. they are merely receding from each other at rates proportional to the distance between them..so very large distances increase at very high rates."
That is, recession is different than movement. Moving at faster than the speed of light would break the laws of physics.

#449221 8 2008-06-28T00:35:03Z incluye
Or you could do this:
Go to the leveldata and find !5^[number]^[number]. The [number]s are the X and Y coordinates.
If you want your thwump coming out of the ground (moving up) subtract 1 from the second [number].
If you want your thwump coming out of the right wall (moving left) subtract 1 from the first [number].
Due to N's arrangement, thwumps placed halfway into the left wall or the ceiling without Z-snapping will move.

Apple is very hostile to creative third-party software and to changing the way it looks or operates, absolutely refuses to allow any other hardware manufacturer to produce OEM machines with their OS, and doesn't even trust their own consumers to change the batteries on their iPhones. On the contrary.
Hypothetically, what if you invented a totally new operating system - let's call it F.I.D.O. - and it's a huge hit. You sell personal computers, music players, phones, and other assorted merchandise. Opposing programmers from other companies want to examine your hardware to see what causes your operating system to run so smoothly and quickly and be so user-friendly. You know, however, that if they could crack the hardware, they could begin to emulate your OS on much less expensive machines, and your market would shrink. You don't let your consumers perform medium-complicated maintenance on their machines, in case they could get into the works.

Quote: Strife, 2008-06-30T22:55:06Z

I happen to think that Vista looks really pretty - why shouldn't I be able to make Mac OS X look like Vista? It's my computer, isn't it?

#454890 12 2008-07-24T20:55:30Z incluye
Quote: wilderness, July 23, 2008 10:45 pm
People tend to think of the rating/click the bitesize first, then justify it.. rather than thinking of what they liked/disliked and applying a rating accordingly. Not always, but enough for the system to become saturated.

Has someone forgotten the former "Unbitesize This Map" button? It wasn't used often, of course, but it was still a good way to go if you thought your decision over and decided the map wasn't worthy.

southpaw had this amazing idea (still trying to find the thread) where he proposed having three Top Rated Pages - one for authors with less than ~15 maps, one for authors with less than ~50, and one for authors above 50. (Or something reasonably along those lines.) The best new authors and relatively new authors would get recognition they wouldn't normally get with one Top Rated page, and since there are loads of new authors, and not as many 15-50 map authors, 15-50-ers would get more ratings as well. The 50 and above would be the best maps of the bunch.

I'd also like to see some kind of Hall of Fame on Newma, where you can sort maps by the number of ratings, tags etc. and maybe even see all the formerly Top Rated maps. Then all the budding N-Artists could see Afternoondragon, new DDA-makers could see Route 666, and action mappers could see maps like Sleeping With a Fish Named Eric. Those maps shouldn't be lost forever, and the Hot Maps page just doesn't work as well - eventually, even for great maps, the submission of new maps will eventually outnumber the frequency of new rates.

My 2.

#455674 575 2008-07-29T02:19:12Z incluye
Quote: Qix, July 29, 2008 01:35 am
Edit: http://www.millerandlevine.com/km/evol/design2/article.html
Another good example of irreducible complexity refuted.

I'd like to quote a few somethings from that site (which, by the way, is obviously neutral. Psh.)

Quote: The site
One of those components, as these quotations indicate, is Factor XII, which initiates the cascade. Once again, however, a nasty little fact gets in the way of intelligent design theory. Dolphins lack Factor XII (Robinson, Kasting, and Aggeler 1969), and yet their blood clots perfectly well. How can this be if the clotting cascade is indeed irreducibly complex? It cannot, of course, and therefore the claim of irreducible complexity is wrong for this system as well.
Behe touched upon this theme in his book.
What I see it saying is, "Oh, I see a problem. Dolphins don't have the chemical that starts the whole process. [They have all the chemicals in the middle, obviously.] Therefore, irreducible complexity must be wrong, because we've found an inconsistency, regardless of where the inconsistency is found."
Why it's important that the initiating chemical is missing: dolphins might have a different chemical that initiates their clotting, in which case this wouldn't be a refutation at all, because if you take away the chemical in the dolphins' blood theirs won't clot either. I don't think that site would mention if the dolphins had another chemical in their blood, though.

Quote: Qix
The lack of a detailed current explanation for a structure, organ, or process does not mean that science will never come up with one. As an example, one might consider the question of how left-right asymmetry arises in vertebrate development, a question that was beyond explanation until the 1990s (Belmonte 1999). In 1990 one might have argued that the body's left-right asymmetry could just as well be explained by the intervention of a designer as by an unknown molecular mechanism. Only a decade later, the actual molecular mechanism was identified (Stern 2002), and any claim one might have made for the intervention of a designer would have been discarded. The same point can be made, of course, regarding any structure or mechanism whose origins are not yet understood.
Again, with that argument, we can't win. All you have to do is argue that you will eventually find a solution to every problem presented to evolution.

Quote: Qix, 2008-07-29T01:35:10Z
Irreducible complexity states that because the evolution of a current system is not understood, it cannot have evolved.

"The characteristic of a system of well-matched, mutually interacting parts performing a specific function, in which the removal of any one of the parts causes the system to cease functioning." -Google
"The system cannot have developed by a series of successive, slight modifications."
I.C. isn't stated from a lack of understanding.

#455831 578 2008-07-29T17:50:24Z incluye
Quote: Atilla, July 29, 2008 02:59 am
Quote: incluye, July 29, 2008 04:58 am
Why doesn't Michael Behe count? What's this supposed refutation to his "irreducible complexity"?
We've been over this before - I believe it starts at about page four of this thread. In short, several things which it has been claimed are "irreducibly complex" have subsequently been shown to have a completely viable path for evolution. Furthermore, humans can produce hardware and software by emulating an evolutionary process - creating some criteria for fitness, creating random designs and testing them against it, then "breeding" or randomly mutating the best performing designs and repeating the process. This can produces results which seem incomprehensible to humans and fit all the requirements for being "irreducibly complex", or even results which look exactly the same as those designed (intelligently, we hope) by human engineers - and yet, we know that they aren't irreducibly complex or human-designed because we know the process by which they were created. In essence, the refutation of the "irreducible complexity" argument is that it has been demonstrated repeatedly that humans cannot reliably determine that something is irreducibly complex, or that it "looks designed", or any other such thing.
Then I suppose it would be tiresome to argue against it - anything comprehensible or reasonable I could raise would immediately be refuted by the "Well, we don't know how it could have evolved yet" argument.

But...wait, that's totally ridiculous. Why would evolution add a useless part? Until the function was added, the part would inhibit, not help, the organism.
Besides, you can't make many "irreducibly complex" systems using that method anyway. The eye would comprehensively screw up with a lens that it had before it was used (which would keep the animal from seeing.)

When that guy made the video about how he showed how the eye evolved, he didn't explain a lot of key concepts - including where the "light-sensitive patch," itself a complicated series of proteins, came from, and why the area with the patch became hollower (normally, the cells would form a nondescript ball unless specifically built, with other chemical helpers, to cave inward).

#455929 582 2008-07-29T22:26:46Z incluye
Quote: Strife, July 29, 2008 09:37 pm
Quote: incluye, 2008-07-29T19:26:10Z
Quote: Demonz, 2008-07-29T19:16:07Z
Not so. Appendices are not necessary at all, they offer some entirely insignificant amount of immunity from diseases and they rupture when infected. Having an appendix does *not* help people overall. Yet appendices still exist - and this is because evolution does not produce perfection. There are ways of making people more efficient, yet evolution has not yet done so because there isn't a lot of evolutionary pressure, and people whose appendices burst nowadays don't usually suffer any in the genepool.
Well, that's operating on the presupposition "Evolution is what really happened."
No, it isn't. However they came about, this is what appendices do. There is no part of Demonz's description which would only be true if appendices had come about through evolution or through creation, so it isn't operating on any other premise than "reality is reality."

Quote: incluye, 2008-07-29T19:26:10Z
However, if IC is correct, we can't tell - because we'll always be saying, "It has to have evolved."
You're assuming that evolutionists will resort to the low, unscientific way of doing things used by proponents of creationism.
This is the primary difference between how science works and how creationism works (or Intelligent Design, if creationism decides to put on a cheap rental suit):
Science will make a hypothesis and then do everything in its power to prove it false. If after years of study it is unable to find the hypothesis false, it reluctantly accepts the hypothesis as a theory, but it will continue to be tested long afterwards, especially as science finds more ways to try to disprove it. If science is continually unsuccessful in falsifying the hypothesis, the hypothesis gets more credit. Evolution is scientific because there is a way to falsify it - if you find an account of an organism simply beginning to exist, or someone proves irreducible complexity beyond a shadow of a doubt, then evolution will be discredited, and science will move on. It is very important for a potential theory to be falsifiable, because that's the only way it can be proven with such a high degree of certainty.
Religion will start with an end goal in mind and then search for supporting evidence, often ignoring or attempting to explain away contradicting evidence with more made-up reasoning (and if that's questioned, things can get recursive). The declared truth will also usually be something that's unfalsifiable (whereas falsifiability is a requirement for scientific theories); the only way to disprove that there was a cosmic creator, for example, is to interrogate the creator. For this reason, it's rejected right from the start as a possible theory by science.
And lastly, science will always admit that a given theory could be wrong and can happily give you a list of things to look into if you want to prove it wrong, but religion will never back down from the truth it's trying to declare and refuses to believe that it could possibly be false.
I've seen exactly - no, really, exactly - the opposite in the real world.

Quote: Strife, 2008-07-29T21:37:26Z
[drivel]
...and that, children, is the real story behind Hell and Satan.
I'm sorry, I really don't find your brand of humor funny. Instead I find it offensive. Could you refrain from making such crude jokes in Serious Discussion?

That article states that the appendix used to be a secondary large intestine used for digesting leaves, which gradually shrank as man ate less and less vegetables and more meat over the years.
You can't argue, though, that that is speculation. It's the possibility that sounds the most viable, and therefore is used as the explanation.

The crux of the matter is that science cannot consider design as a possibility for the origin of life. It involves the supernatural, which is not considered. Therefore, even if ID is what really happened, it will never be determined.

Oh, and one more thing:

Quote: Strife, 2008-07-29T21:37:26Z

||||,_icon,x_offset^y_offset^url

#21427 1 2008-09-11T16:58:04Z incluye
Technically, adaptation doesn't work for evolution, because whatever physical traits one achieves while living does not change the reproductive DNA.

Say that a man is a champion bodybuilder. He has some of the largest and strongest muscles in the world. So when his baby son is born, is the kid going to have huge muscles? Of course not. Likewise with stronger jaws after a lifetime of chewing meat instead of plants, and a lifetime of not using a secondary large intestine after eating meat instead of plants.

Anyway, I'm locking this thread, because this is a thought that occurred to me that I don't want to escalate into a heated debate. If you want to counter it, PM me.
